========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi. Um, well, what brings me in is that I just can't seem to shake this really heavy mix of fear and guilt lately. It's exhausting. I mean, my job is stressful enough on its own—I'm a building inspector, and you wouldn't believe the corners these contractors try to cut. Just Tuesday I was out at that new commercial site on 8th Avenue, and they tried to cover up some serious structural flaws with drywall before I even signed off on the framing. I had to red-tag the whole site. The foreman was furious, but, you know, safety first. If I don't catch it, who will? 

But anyway, I'm just... I'm tense all day now. It's like I'm constantly waiting for something else to go wrong, and I just keep blaming myself for not stopping what happened. I don't know. My mom always said I carry the weight of the world on my shoulders. She actually just retired and moved down to Florida, which is nice for her, she got this cute little condo near the beach... but yeah, I just can't shake this awful feeling.

Oh, no, it wasn't the job. I mean, work has always been a headache, but this is different. This has been going on for about four months, ever since my husband's fall during the porch repair. The previous owners just let the weather completely destroy the back steps, and we’ve been trying to do the repairs ourselves on the weekends to save some money. Have you seen the price of lumber lately? It is absolutely ridiculous. We went to the hardware store for just a few boards of treated pine and I couldn't believe the total at the register. [
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n2\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1 Generalized Anxiety Disorder, F43.22 Adjustment Disorder with Anxiety, F43.23 Adjustment Disorder with Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, F32.0 Major Depressive Disorder, Mild Episode, F60.7 Obsessive-Compulsive Personality Disorder (possible underlying trait)\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Um, well, what brings me in is that I just can't seem to shake this really heavy mix of fear and guilt lately. It's exhausting. I mean, my job is stressful enough on its own—I'm a building inspector, and you wouldn't believe the corners these contractors try to cut. Just Tuesday I was out at that new commercial site on 8th Avenue, and they tried to cover up some serious structural flaws with drywall before I even signed off on the framing. I had to red-tag the whole site. The foreman was furious, but, you know, safety first. If I don't catch it, who will? \n\nBut anyway, I'm just... I'm tense all day now. It's like I'm constantly waiting for something else to go wrong, and I just keep blaming myself for not stopping what happened. I don't know. My mom always said I carry the weight of the world on my shoulders. She actually just retired and moved down to Florida, which is nice for her, she got this cute little condo near the beach... but yeah, I just can't shake this awful feeling.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way? Have these feelings of tension, fear, and guilt been constant, or did they start around the time of these recent job-related stressors?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, it wasn't the job. I mean, work has always been a headache, but this is different. This has been going on for about four months, ever since my husband's fall during the porch repair. My husband finally called them and argued with the guy for like twenty minutes, but they wouldn't budge. So we went with a different company and ended up saving like forty bucks a month, which is great because we really need to replace the gutters before winter. The leaves from the neighbor's oak tree just completely clog them up every single fall, and I swear, that tree drops more branches than leaves sometimes. But no, I'm not just anxious about random things. It's just... this heavy feeling about the accident.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about the accident and whether you could have done something differently, even when you’re not actively thinking about it?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I do worry about that a lot. I mean, it's not just about the accident itself, but what it means about me. I keep thinking that if I missed something so obvious at my own house, then maybe I'm not as careful or competent as I thought I was. Which is crazy, because my work reports are always spotless. I mean, my supervisor, Dave, he just did my annual review last month and gave me the highest marks in attention to detail. Dave's a good guy, actually, he just had his first grandbaby—a little girl. He showed me pictures on his phone the other day, and she was wearing this tiny little knitted hat that his wife made. She knits all kinds of things, apparently she sells them at the farmer's market on weekends. I've been meaning to go check out that market, they're supposed to have really good local honey, and I've heard local honey is great for seasonal allergies. My allergies have been acting up so bad lately with all the ragweed. But anyway, yeah, even though my work is fine, I just have this awful belief now that I'm somehow failing at being safe.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any trouble focusing or staying concentrated on tasks lately, even ones you usually handle without a problem?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. My focus is actually pretty good. I mean, as a building inspector, I have to be pretty dialed in, right? Like, I was just out at that new mixed-use development they're putting up over on 4th Street the other day. Have you seen the traffic around there lately? It is an absolute nightmare. They blocked off two whole lanes just for the cement trucks, and the detour signs make zero sense. I ended up stuck behind this huge delivery truck for like twenty minutes, and the driver was just sitting there scrolling on his phone the whole time. It's crazy how glued to their screens people are now. My sister just got my niece a smartphone for her tenth birthday, which honestly just seems way too young to me. When we were ten, we were outside riding our bikes around the neighborhood until the streetlights came on, you know? We used to put those little plastic beads on the spokes that made noise when you pedaled. But yeah, anyway, my concentration at work and stuff hasn't been an issue at all.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you found yourself avoiding certain places, people, or activities because they remind you of your husband's fall?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 31 / construction_or_building_inspector / associates\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Trauma_Avoidance\nmanifestation: \"I've started turning down jobs with raised porches or second-story decks, and at home I find excuses not to talk about the repair or look at the spot where it happened.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yeah, I guess I have been avoiding some things. Like at work, I've started turning down jobs that have raised porches or second-story decks. I just... I can't do it right now. Which is annoying because the weather has been so perfect for outdoor inspections lately. We had that string of days last week where it was just sunny and 70 degrees, like absolute perfection. I love that kind of weather, it reminds me of this trip my husband and I took to San Diego a few years ago. We stayed at this little Airbnb near the beach, and the host left us this basket of local snacks. There were these incredible chili-lime macadamia nuts in there—I've actually tried looking for them online since we got back, but I can never find the exact brand. I did find a recipe to make them at home, but honestly, who has the time to roast their own nuts? Especially with how expensive groceries are right now. I went to the store just to grab eggs and milk yesterday and it was like twenty dollars. But anyway, at home, I also just find excuses not to talk about the repair or even look at the spot where he fell. I just try to keep busy with other stuff so I don't have to deal with it.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, the guilt is definitely there. I mean, that's really what brings me in, honestly. I just can't shake this heavy mix of fear and guilt, like I'm just tense all day waiting for something else to go wrong, and I keep blaming myself for not stopping what happened. It's exhausting. I was telling my friend Sarah about it—she's a nurse over at the county hospital, actually. She works in the pediatric ward, which I always tell her I could never do. Kids are just so fragile, you know? She was telling me about this little boy who came in last week who swallowed a bunch of those magnetic toy beads. Can you believe they even still sell those? I swear, half the toys they make nowadays seem like hazards. When I was a kid, my favorite toy was this wooden train set my grandfather carved for me. He was amazing with woodworking. He had this whole shop set up in his garage with a lathe and everything. The smell of sawdust always reminds me of him. He used to make these intricate little birdhouses, too. I actually have one hanging in my backyard right now, and we get the most beautiful blue jays visiting it. They're so loud, though, especially early in the morning. But yeah, the guilt is just... it's a lot to carry around all the time.

But yeah, anyway, my concentration at work and stuff hasn't been an issue at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself avoiding certain places, people, or activities because they remind you of your husband's fall?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I guess I have been avoiding some things. Like at work, I've started turning down jobs that have raised porches or second-story decks. I just... I can't do it right now. Which is annoying because the weather has been so perfect for outdoor inspections lately. We had that string of days last week where it was just sunny and 70 degrees, like absolute perfection. I love that kind of weather, it reminds me of this trip my husband and I took to San Diego a few years ago. We stayed at this little Airbnb near the beach, and the host left us this basket of local snacks. There were these incredible chili-lime macadamia nuts in there—I've actually tried looking for them online since we got back, but I can never find the exact brand. I did find a recipe to make them at home, but honestly, who has the time to roast their own nuts? Especially with how expensive groceries are right now. I went to the store just to grab eggs and milk yesterday and it was like twenty dollars. But anyway, at home, I also just find excuses not to talk about the repair or even look at the spot where he fell. I just try to keep busy with other stuff so I don't have to deal with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling guilty or blaming yourself for your husband's fall, even though you know it may not have been entirely your fault?\n\nPatient: Yeah, the guilt is definitely there. I mean, that's really what brings me in, honestly. I just can't shake this heavy mix of fear and guilt, like I'm just tense all day waiting for something else to go wrong, and I keep blaming myself for not stopping what happened. It's exhausting. I was telling my friend Sarah about it—she's a nurse over at the county hospital, actually. She works in the pediatric ward, which I always tell her I could never do. Kids are just so fragile, you know? She was telling me about this little boy who came in last week who swallowed a bunch of those magnetic toy beads. Can you believe they even still sell those? I swear, half the toys they make nowadays seem like hazards. When I was a kid, my favorite toy was this wooden train set my grandfather carved for me. He was amazing with woodworking. He had this whole shop set up in his garage with a lathe and everything. The smell of sawdust always reminds me of him. He used to make these intricate little birdhouses, too. I actually have one hanging in my backyard right now, and we get the most beautiful blue jays visiting it. They're so loud, though, especially early in the morning. But yeah, the guilt is just... it's a lot to carry around all the time.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like your guilt and fear are directly related to the accident and your role in it, or do you sometimes feel this way even when you're not thinking about the accident?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I mean, it's definitely tied to the accident. I don't really walk around feeling guilty about other stuff, you know? It's just... it's about him falling and me not fixing that railing when I should have. Which is crazy because I literally inspect buildings for a living. I should have known better. I was trying to fry some chicken, which I never do, but I'd watched this cooking show the night before and got inspired. The host made it look so easy. Have you ever noticed how cooking shows always have all the ingredients perfectly chopped in those little glass bowls before they even start? It's like, of course cooking is relaxing when you have an entire prep team behind the scenes. I bought a set of those little glass bowls thinking it would make me more organized, but they just end up taking up space in my dishwasher. I hate unloading the dishwasher, honestly. But no, I'm not just anxious about random things. It's just... this heavy feeling about the accident.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about the accident and whether you could have done something differently, even when you’re not actively thinking about it?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I do worry about that a lot. I mean, my supervisor, Dave, he just did my annual review last month and gave me the highest marks in attention to detail. Dave's a good guy, actually, he just had his first grandbaby—a little girl. No, not really. It's pretty much just tied to the accident. If I'm not thinking about the porch or him falling, I don't just sit around feeling scared out of nowhere. Yeah, sleep is... it's really bad right now. Most nights I just lie there replaying things in my head for an hour or two before I even drift off. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Um, well, what brings me in is that I just can't seem to shake this really heavy mix of fear and guilt lately. It's exhausting. Just Tuesday I was out at that new commercial site on 8th Avenue, and they tried to cover up some serious structural flaws with drywall before I even signed off on the framing. I had to red-tag the whole site. The foreman was furious, but, you know, safety first. If I don't catch it, who will? \n\nBut anyway, I'm just... I'm tense all day now. It's like I'm constantly waiting for something else to go wrong, and I just keep blaming myself for not stopping what happened. I don't know. We were fixing up the back deck because we bought this older Craftsman house a couple of years ago, and it has all this gorgeous original woodwork, but honestly, it’s been such a money pit. The previous owners just let the weather completely destroy the back steps, and we’ve been trying to do the repairs ourselves on the weekends to save some money. Have you seen the price of lumber lately? It is absolutely ridiculous. We went to the hardware store for just a few boards of treated pine and I couldn't believe the total at the register. But yeah, anyway... it's been four months. Ever since he fell out there, I just haven't been able to shake this feeling.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your worry or anxiety extends beyond just work or your husband’s accident—like if you're feeling anxious about a lot of different things, even when there isn’t a clear reason?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say it's about everything. It's really just tied to what happened. I don't sit around worrying about, you know, getting a flat tire or whether the bills are paid on time. We actually just switched our car insurance because they kept hiking the rates up every six months for no reason. My husband finally called them and argued with the guy for like twenty minutes, but they wouldn't budge. So we went with a different company and ended up saving like forty bucks a month, which is great because we really need to replace the gutters before winter. The leaves from the neighbor's oak tree just completely clog them up every single fall, and I swear, that tree drops more branches than leaves sometimes. But no, I'm not just anxious about random things. It's just... this heavy feeling about the accident.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about the accident and whether you could have done something differently, even when you’re not actively thinking about it?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I do worry about that a lot. I mean, it's not just about the accident itself, but what it means about me. I keep thinking that if I missed something so obvious at my own house, then maybe I'm not as careful or competent as I thought I was. Which is crazy, because my work reports are always spotless. I mean, my supervisor, Dave, he just did my annual review last month and gave me the highest marks in attention to detail. Dave's a good guy, actually, he just had his first grandbaby—a little girl. He showed me pictures on his phone the other day, and she was wearing this tiny little knitted hat that his wife made. She knits all kinds of things, apparently she sells them at the farmer's market on weekends. I've been meaning to go check out that market, they're supposed to have really good local honey, and I've heard local honey is great for seasonal allergies. My allergies have been acting up so bad lately with all the ragweed. But anyway, yeah, even though my work is fine, I just have this awful belief now that I'm somehow failing at being safe.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any trouble focusing or staying concentrated on tasks lately, even ones you usually handle without a problem?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. My focus is actually pretty good. I mean, as a building inspector, I have to be pretty dialed in, right? Like, I was just out at that new mixed-use development they're putting up over on 4th Street the other day. Have you seen the traffic around there lately? It is an absolute nightmare. They blocked off two whole lanes just for the cement trucks, and the detour signs make zero sense. I ended up stuck behind this huge delivery truck for like twenty minutes, and the driver was just sitting there scrolling on his phone the whole time. It's crazy how glued to their screens people are now. My sister just got my niece a smartphone for her tenth birthday, which honestly just seems way too young to me. When we were ten, we were outside riding our bikes around the neighborhood until the streetlights came on, you know? We used to put those little plastic beads on the spokes that made noise when you pedaled. But yeah, anyway, my concentration at work and stuff hasn't been an issue at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself avoiding certain places, people, or activities because they remind you of your husband's fall?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I guess I have been avoiding some things. Like at work, I've started turning down jobs that have raised porches or second-story decks. I just... I can't do it right now. Which is annoying because the weather has been so perfect for outdoor inspections lately. We had that string of days last week where it was just sunny and 70 degrees, like absolute perfection. I love that kind of weather, it reminds me of this trip my husband and I took to San Diego a few years ago. We stayed at this little Airbnb near the beach, and the host left us this basket of local snacks. There were these incredible chili-lime macadamia nuts in there—I've actually tried looking for them online since we got back, but I can never find the exact brand. I did find a recipe to make them at home, but honestly, who has the time to roast their own nuts? Especially with how expensive groceries are right now. I went to the store just to grab eggs and milk yesterday and it was like twenty dollars. But anyway, at home, I also just find excuses not to talk about the repair or even look at the spot where he fell. I just try to keep busy with other stuff so I don't have to deal with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling guilty or blaming yourself for your husband's fall, even though you know it may not have been entirely your fault?\n\nPatient: Yeah, the guilt is definitely there. I mean, that's really what brings me in, honestly. I just can't shake this heavy mix of fear and guilt, like I'm just tense all day waiting for something else to go wrong, and I keep blaming myself for not stopping what happened. It's exhausting. I was telling my friend Sarah about it—she's a nurse over at the county hospital, actually. She works in the pediatric ward, which I always tell her I could never do. Kids are just so fragile, you know? She was telling me about this little boy who came in last week who swallowed a bunch of those magnetic toy beads. Can you believe they even still sell those? I swear, half the toys they make nowadays seem like hazards. When I was a kid, my favorite toy was this wooden train set my grandfather carved for me. He was amazing with woodworking. He had this whole shop set up in his garage with a lathe and everything. The smell of sawdust always reminds me of him. He used to make these intricate little birdhouses, too. I actually have one hanging in my backyard right now, and we get the most beautiful blue jays visiting it. They're so loud, though, especially early in the morning. But yeah, the guilt is just... it's a lot to carry around all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your guilt and fear are directly related to the accident and your role in it, or do you sometimes feel this way even when you're not thinking about the accident?\n\nPatient: I mean, it's definitely tied to the accident. I don't really walk around feeling guilty about other stuff, you know? It's just... it's about him falling and me not fixing that railing when I should have. Which is crazy because I literally inspect buildings for a living. I should have known better. I did this inspection on a commercial property last month, this old warehouse they're converting into lofts. The amount of code violations was unbelievable. They had exposed wiring just hanging right next to the water pipes. I was talking to the general contractor, and he was trying to tell me it was \"temporary.\" Like, buddy, temporary or not, it's a fire hazard. I actually had a small grease fire in my kitchen once, years ago. Scared the life out of me. I was trying to fry some chicken, which I never do, but I'd watched this cooking show the night before and got inspired. The host made it look so easy. Have you ever noticed how cooking shows always have all the ingredients perfectly chopped in those little glass bowls before they even start? It's like, of course cooking is relaxing when you have an entire prep team behind the scenes. I bought a set of those little glass bowls thinking it would make me more organized, but they just end up taking up space in my dishwasher. I hate unloading the dishwasher, honestly. It's my least favorite chore. But yeah, the fear and the guilt... it's all just about the accident. It's not just a general thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or fearful even when you're not thinking about the accident or anything related to it?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's pretty much just tied to the accident. If I'm not thinking about the porch or him falling, I don't just sit around feeling scared out of nowhere. I mean, unless I'm watching a scary movie or something, but I don't really do that anymore. I used to love horror movies when I was a teenager. My friends and I would rent those cheesy slasher films from Blockbuster—God, remember Blockbuster? Getting to walk down those aisles on a Friday night, smelling the plastic cases and the popcorn. It was such a ritual. Now everything is just streaming, and you spend more time scrolling through options than actually watching anything. I tried to find something to watch on Netflix the other night and gave up after twenty minutes. I ended up just watching this documentary about octopuses instead. Did you know they have three hearts? It's fascinating. But they only live for like a year or two, which seems so unfair for how smart they are. I read somewhere that if they lived longer, they could probably take over the world. Honestly, they'd probably do a better job than we are. But no, to answer your question, the anxiety is just about what happened with my husband.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel physically on edge or notice things like trouble sleeping, irritability, or being easily startled, especially since the accident?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sleep is... it's really bad right now. Most nights I just lie there replaying things in my head for an hour or two before I even drift off. And even when I finally do fall asleep, I wake up at every little creak the house makes, or I have these awful dreams where I hear him hit the ground again. It's miserable. I actually tried drinking some chamomile tea before bed, thinking it might help. My neighbor, Mrs. Higgins, swears by it. She grows her own chamomile in this little garden box on her patio. She's got quite the green thumb, honestly. She grew these massive heirloom tomatoes last summer that she shared with us—they were so good, just sliced with a little salt and olive oil. I tried growing tomatoes once, but the squirrels got to every single one of them before they even ripened. They are so bold! I saw one sitting on my fence just staring at me while it ate a strawberry right out of my planter. I bought this supposedly squirrel-proof bird feeder, but they figured it out in like two days. They're like little acrobats. I watched a video online about this guy who built this elaborate obstacle course for the squirrels in his yard, and they just breezed right through it. But anyway, yeah, the sleeping thing is a real problem since the accident. I'm just so on edge all night.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I bought a set of those little glass bowls thinking it would make me more organized, but they just end up taking up space in my dishwasher. I hate unloading the dishwasher, honestly. It's my least favorite chore. But yeah, the fear and the guilt... it's all just about the accident. It's not just a general thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or fearful even when you're not thinking about the accident or anything related to it?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's pretty much just tied to the accident. If I'm not thinking about the porch or him falling, I don't just sit around feeling scared out of nowhere. I mean, unless I'm watching a scary movie or something, but I don't really do that anymore. I used to love horror movies when I was a teenager. My friends and I would rent those cheesy slasher films from Blockbuster—God, remember Blockbuster? Getting to walk down those aisles on a Friday night, smelling the plastic cases and the popcorn. It was such a ritual. Now everything is just streaming, and you spend more time scrolling through options than actually watching anything. I tried to find something to watch on Netflix the other night and gave up after twenty minutes. I ended up just watching this documentary about octopuses instead. Did you know they have three hearts? It's fascinating. But they only live for like a year or two, which seems so unfair for how smart they are. I read somewhere that if they lived longer, they could probably take over the world. Honestly, they'd probably do a better job than we are. But no, to answer your question, the anxiety is just about what happened with my husband.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel physically on edge or notice things like trouble sleeping, irritability, or being easily startled, especially since the accident?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sleep is... it's really bad right now. Most nights I just lie there replaying things in my head for an hour or two before I even drift off. And even when I finally do fall asleep, I wake up at every little creak the house makes, or I have these awful dreams where I hear him hit the ground again. It's miserable. I actually tried drinking some chamomile tea before bed, thinking it might help. My neighbor, Mrs. Higgins, swears by it. She grows her own chamomile in this little garden box on her patio. She's got quite the green thumb, honestly. She grew these massive heirloom tomatoes last summer that she shared with us—they were so good, just sliced with a little salt and olive oil. I tried growing tomatoes once, but the squirrels got to every single one of them before they even ripened. They are so bold! I saw one sitting on my fence just staring at me while it ate a strawberry right out of my planter. I bought this supposedly squirrel-proof bird feeder, but they figured it out in like two days. They're like little acrobats. I watched a video online about this guy who built this elaborate obstacle course for the squirrels in his yard, and they just breezed right through it. But anyway, yeah, the sleeping thing is a real problem since the accident. I'm just so on edge all night.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever notice feelings of anxiety or fear coming up at times when your mind is completely off the accident and not thinking about it at all?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. No, I really don't. Like I was saying, it's pretty much just when I'm thinking about the porch or him falling. If my mind is actually off it, which is rare, I feel okay. Like the other day, I was at the grocery store, just trying to find this specific brand of mustard my husband likes. It's this stone-ground one with the little seeds in it. He closed down during the pandemic, though, which is such a shame. A lot of good places closed down then. It's crazy how much the neighborhood has changed just in the last few years. But yeah, no, if I'm not thinking about the accident, I don't just feel anxious for no reason.

But no, I'm not just anxious about random things. It's just... this heavy feeling about the accident.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about the accident and whether you could have done something differently, even when you’re not actively thinking about it?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I do worry about that a lot. I mean, it's not just about the accident itself, but what it means about me. I keep thinking that if I missed something so obvious at my own house, then maybe I'm not as careful or competent as I thought I was. Which is crazy, because my work reports are always spotless. I mean, my supervisor, Dave, he just did my annual review last month and gave me the highest marks in attention to detail. Dave's a good guy, actually, he just had his first grandbaby—a little girl. He showed me pictures on his phone the other day, and she was wearing this tiny little knitted hat that his wife made. She knits all kinds of things, apparently she sells them at the farmer's market on weekends. I've been meaning to go check out that market, they're supposed to have really good local honey, and I've heard local honey is great for seasonal allergies. My allergies have been acting up so bad lately with all the ragweed. But anyway, yeah, even though my work is fine, I just have this awful belief now that I'm somehow failing at being safe.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any trouble focusing or staying concentrated on tasks lately, even ones you usually handle without a problem?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. My focus is actually pretty good. I mean, as a building inspector, I have to be pretty dialed in, right? Like, I was just out at that new mixed-use development they're putting up over on 4th Street the other day. Have you seen the traffic around there lately? It is an absolute nightmare. They blocked off two whole lanes just for the cement trucks, and the detour signs make zero sense. I ended up stuck behind this huge delivery truck for like twenty minutes, and the driver was just sitting there scrolling on his phone the whole time. It's crazy how glued to their screens people are now. My sister just got my niece a smartphone for her tenth birthday, which honestly just seems way too young to me. When we were ten, we were outside riding our bikes around the neighborhood until the streetlights came on, you know? We used to put those little plastic beads on the spokes that made noise when you pedaled. But yeah, anyway, my concentration at work and stuff hasn't been an issue at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself avoiding certain places, people, or activities because they remind you of your husband's fall?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I guess I have been avoiding some things. Like at work, I've started turning down jobs that have raised porches or second-story decks. I just... I can't do it right now. Which is annoying because the weather has been so perfect for outdoor inspections lately. We had that string of days last week where it was just sunny and 70 degrees, like absolute perfection. I love that kind of weather, it reminds me of this trip my husband and I took to San Diego a few years ago. We stayed at this little Airbnb near the beach, and the host left us this basket of local snacks. There were these incredible chili-lime macadamia nuts in there—I've actually tried looking for them online since we got back, but I can never find the exact brand. I did find a recipe to make them at home, but honestly, who has the time to roast their own nuts? Especially with how expensive groceries are right now. I went to the store just to grab eggs and milk yesterday and it was like twenty dollars. But anyway, at home, I also just find excuses not to talk about the repair or even look at the spot where he fell. I just try to keep busy with other stuff so I don't have to deal with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling guilty or blaming yourself for your husband's fall, even though you know it may not have been entirely your fault?\n\nPatient: Yeah, the guilt is definitely there. I mean, that's really what brings me in, honestly. I just can't shake this heavy mix of fear and guilt, like I'm just tense all day waiting for something else to go wrong, and I keep blaming myself for not stopping what happened. It's exhausting. I was telling my friend Sarah about it—she's a nurse over at the county hospital, actually. She works in the pediatric ward, which I always tell her I could never do. Kids are just so fragile, you know? She was telling me about this little boy who came in last week who swallowed a bunch of those magnetic toy beads. Can you believe they even still sell those? I swear, half the toys they make nowadays seem like hazards. When I was a kid, my favorite toy was this wooden train set my grandfather carved for me. He was amazing with woodworking. He had this whole shop set up in his garage with a lathe and everything. The smell of sawdust always reminds me of him. He used to make these intricate little birdhouses, too. I actually have one hanging in my backyard right now, and we get the most beautiful blue jays visiting it. They're so loud, though, especially early in the morning. But yeah, the guilt is just... it's a lot to carry around all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your guilt and fear are directly related to the accident and your role in it, or do you sometimes feel this way even when you're not thinking about the accident?\n\nPatient: I mean, it's definitely tied to the accident. I don't really walk around feeling guilty about other stuff, you know? It's just... it's about him falling and me not fixing that railing when I should have. Which is crazy because I literally inspect buildings for a living. I should have known better. I did this inspection on a commercial property last month, this old warehouse they're converting into lofts. The amount of code violations was unbelievable. They had exposed wiring just hanging right next to the water pipes. I was talking to the general contractor, and he was trying to tell me it was \"temporary.\" Like, buddy, temporary or not, it's a fire hazard. I actually had a small grease fire in my kitchen once, years ago. Scared the life out of me. I was trying to fry some chicken, which I never do, but I'd watched this cooking show the night before and got inspired. The host made it look so easy. Have you ever noticed how cooking shows always have all the ingredients perfectly chopped in those little glass bowls before they even start? It's like, of course cooking is relaxing when you have an entire prep team behind the scenes. I bought a set of those little glass bowls thinking it would make me more organized, but they just end up taking up space in my dishwasher. I hate unloading the dishwasher, honestly. It's my least favorite chore. But yeah, the fear and the guilt... it's all just about the accident. It's not just a general thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or fearful even when you're not thinking about the accident or anything related to it?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's pretty much just tied to the accident. If I'm not thinking about the porch or him falling, I don't just sit around feeling scared out of nowhere. I mean, unless I'm watching a scary movie or something, but I don't really do that anymore. I used to love horror movies when I was a teenager. My friends and I would rent those cheesy slasher films from Blockbuster—God, remember Blockbuster? Getting to walk down those aisles on a Friday night, smelling the plastic cases and the popcorn. It was such a ritual. Now everything is just streaming, and you spend more time scrolling through options than actually watching anything. I tried to find something to watch on Netflix the other night and gave up after twenty minutes. I ended up just watching this documentary about octopuses instead. Did you know they have three hearts? It's fascinating. But they only live for like a year or two, which seems so unfair for how smart they are. I read somewhere that if they lived longer, they could probably take over the world. Honestly, they'd probably do a better job than we are. But no, to answer your question, the anxiety is just about what happened with my husband.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel physically on edge or notice things like trouble sleeping, irritability, or being easily startled, especially since the accident?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sleep is... it's really bad right now. Most nights I just lie there replaying things in my head for an hour or two before I even drift off. And even when I finally do fall asleep, I wake up at every little creak the house makes, or I have these awful dreams where I hear him hit the ground again. It's miserable. I actually tried drinking some chamomile tea before bed, thinking it might help. My neighbor, Mrs. Higgins, swears by it. She grows her own chamomile in this little garden box on her patio. She's got quite the green thumb, honestly. She grew these massive heirloom tomatoes last summer that she shared with us—they were so good, just sliced with a little salt and olive oil. I tried growing tomatoes once, but the squirrels got to every single one of them before they even ripened. They are so bold! I saw one sitting on my fence just staring at me while it ate a strawberry right out of my planter. I bought this supposedly squirrel-proof bird feeder, but they figured it out in like two days. They're like little acrobats. I watched a video online about this guy who built this elaborate obstacle course for the squirrels in his yard, and they just breezed right through it. But anyway, yeah, the sleeping thing is a real problem since the accident. I'm just so on edge all night.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever notice feelings of anxiety or fear coming up at times when your mind is completely off the accident and not thinking about it at all?\n\nPatient: No, I really don't. Like I was saying, it's pretty much just when I'm thinking about the porch or him falling. If my mind is actually off it, which is rare, I feel okay. Like the other day, I was at the grocery store, just trying to find this specific brand of mustard my husband likes. It's this stone-ground one with the little seeds in it. I don't really get the appeal, honestly, I'm more of a classic yellow mustard person. Or maybe honey mustard if I'm having chicken nuggets. I haven't had chicken nuggets in forever. There used to be this place near my old apartment that made the best homemade chicken nuggets, they were huge. The guy who owned it was this really grumpy older man, but he made amazing food. He closed down during the pandemic, though, which is such a shame. A lot of good places closed down then. It's crazy how much the neighborhood has changed just in the last few years. All these new coffee shops popping up everywhere, charging like seven dollars for a latte. I mean, I like a good latte, but that's just ridiculous. I bought an espresso machine for my house thinking it would save me money, but it's so complicated to use. It takes me twenty minutes just to make one cup, and then I have to clean the little milk frother thing, which is a huge pain. So it just sits on my counter taking up space. But yeah, no, if I'm not thinking about the accident, I don't just feel anxious for no reason.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Have these feelings of tension, fear, and guilt been constant, or did they start around the time of these recent job-related stressors?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, it wasn't the job. I mean, work has always been a headache, but this is different. This has been going on for about four months, ever since my husband's fall during the porch repair. We were fixing up the back deck because we bought this older Craftsman house a couple of years ago, and it has all this gorgeous original woodwork, but honestly, it’s been such a money pit. The previous owners just let the weather completely destroy the back steps, and we’ve been trying to do the repairs ourselves on the weekends to save some money. Have you seen the price of lumber lately? It is absolutely ridiculous. We went to the hardware store for just a few boards of treated pine and I couldn't believe the total at the register. But yeah, anyway... it's been four months. Ever since he fell out there, I just haven't been able to shake this feeling.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your worry or anxiety extends beyond just work or your husband’s accident—like if you're feeling anxious about a lot of different things, even when there isn’t a clear reason?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say it's about everything. It's really just tied to what happened. I don't sit around worrying about, you know, getting a flat tire or whether the bills are paid on time. We actually just switched our car insurance because they kept hiking the rates up every six months for no reason. My husband finally called them and argued with the guy for like twenty minutes, but they wouldn't budge. So we went with a different company and ended up saving like forty bucks a month, which is great because we really need to replace the gutters before winter. The leaves from the neighbor's oak tree just completely clog them up every single fall, and I swear, that tree drops more branches than leaves sometimes. But no, I'm not just anxious about random things. It's just... this heavy feeling about the accident.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about the accident and whether you could have done something differently, even when you’re not actively thinking about it?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I do worry about that a lot. I mean, it's not just about the accident itself, but what it means about me. I keep thinking that if I missed something so obvious at my own house, then maybe I'm not as careful or competent as I thought I was. Which is crazy, because my work reports are always spotless. I mean, my supervisor, Dave, he just did my annual review last month and gave me the highest marks in attention to detail. Dave's a good guy, actually, he just had his first grandbaby—a little girl. He showed me pictures on his phone the other day, and she was wearing this tiny little knitted hat that his wife made. She knits all kinds of things, apparently she sells them at the farmer's market on weekends. I've been meaning to go check out that market, they're supposed to have really good local honey, and I've heard local honey is great for seasonal allergies. My allergies have been acting up so bad lately with all the ragweed. But anyway, yeah, even though my work is fine, I just have this awful belief now that I'm somehow failing at being safe.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any trouble focusing or staying concentrated on tasks lately, even ones you usually handle without a problem?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. My focus is actually pretty good. I mean, as a building inspector, I have to be pretty dialed in, right? Like, I was just out at that new mixed-use development they're putting up over on 4th Street the other day. Have you seen the traffic around there lately? It is an absolute nightmare. They blocked off two whole lanes just for the cement trucks, and the detour signs make zero sense. I ended up stuck behind this huge delivery truck for like twenty minutes, and the driver was just sitting there scrolling on his phone the whole time. It's crazy how glued to their screens people are now. My sister just got my niece a smartphone for her tenth birthday, which honestly just seems way too young to me. When we were ten, we were outside riding our bikes around the neighborhood until the streetlights came on, you know? We used to put those little plastic beads on the spokes that made noise when you pedaled. But yeah, anyway, my concentration at work and stuff hasn't been an issue at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself avoiding certain places, people, or activities because they remind you of your husband's fall?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I guess I have been avoiding some things. Like at work, I've started turning down jobs that have raised porches or second-story decks. I just... I can't do it right now. Which is annoying because the weather has been so perfect for outdoor inspections lately. We had that string of days last week where it was just sunny and 70 degrees, like absolute perfection. I love that kind of weather, it reminds me of this trip my husband and I took to San Diego a few years ago. We stayed at this little Airbnb near the beach, and the host left us this basket of local snacks. There were these incredible chili-lime macadamia nuts in there—I've actually tried looking for them online since we got back, but I can never find the exact brand. I did find a recipe to make them at home, but honestly, who has the time to roast their own nuts? Especially with how expensive groceries are right now. I went to the store just to grab eggs and milk yesterday and it was like twenty dollars. But anyway, at home, I also just find excuses not to talk about the repair or even look at the spot where he fell. I just try to keep busy with other stuff so I don't have to deal with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling guilty or blaming yourself for your husband's fall, even though you know it may not have been entirely your fault?\n\nPatient: Yeah, the guilt is definitely there. I mean, that's really what brings me in, honestly. I just can't shake this heavy mix of fear and guilt, like I'm just tense all day waiting for something else to go wrong, and I keep blaming myself for not stopping what happened. It's exhausting. I was telling my friend Sarah about it—she's a nurse over at the county hospital, actually. She works in the pediatric ward, which I always tell her I could never do. Kids are just so fragile, you know? She was telling me about this little boy who came in last week who swallowed a bunch of those magnetic toy beads. Can you believe they even still sell those? I swear, half the toys they make nowadays seem like hazards. When I was a kid, my favorite toy was this wooden train set my grandfather carved for me. He was amazing with woodworking. He had this whole shop set up in his garage with a lathe and everything. The smell of sawdust always reminds me of him. He used to make these intricate little birdhouses, too. I actually have one hanging in my backyard right now, and we get the most beautiful blue jays visiting it. They're so loud, though, especially early in the morning. But yeah, the guilt is just... it's a lot to carry around all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your guilt and fear are directly related to the accident and your role in it, or do you sometimes feel this way even when you're not thinking about the accident?\n\nPatient: I mean, it's definitely tied to the accident. I don't really walk around feeling guilty about other stuff, you know? It's just... it's about him falling and me not fixing that railing when I should have. Which is crazy because I literally inspect buildings for a living. I should have known better. I did this inspection on a commercial property last month, this old warehouse they're converting into lofts. The amount of code violations was unbelievable. They had exposed wiring just hanging right next to the water pipes. I was talking to the general contractor, and he was trying to tell me it was \"temporary.\" Like, buddy, temporary or not, it's a fire hazard. I actually had a small grease fire in my kitchen once, years ago. Scared the life out of me. I was trying to fry some chicken, which I never do, but I'd watched this cooking show the night before and got inspired. The host made it look so easy. Have you ever noticed how cooking shows always have all the ingredients perfectly chopped in those little glass bowls before they even start? It's like, of course cooking is relaxing when you have an entire prep team behind the scenes. I bought a set of those little glass bowls thinking it would make me more organized, but they just end up taking up space in my dishwasher. I hate unloading the dishwasher, honestly. It's my least favorite chore. But yeah, the fear and the guilt... it's all just about the accident. It's not just a general thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or fearful even when you're not thinking about the accident or anything related to it?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's pretty much just tied to the accident. If I'm not thinking about the porch or him falling, I don't just sit around feeling scared out of nowhere. I mean, unless I'm watching a scary movie or something, but I don't really do that anymore. I used to love horror movies when I was a teenager. My friends and I would rent those cheesy slasher films from Blockbuster—God, remember Blockbuster? Getting to walk down those aisles on a Friday night, smelling the plastic cases and the popcorn. It was such a ritual. Now everything is just streaming, and you spend more time scrolling through options than actually watching anything. I tried to find something to watch on Netflix the other night and gave up after twenty minutes. I ended up just watching this documentary about octopuses instead. Did you know they have three hearts? It's fascinating. But they only live for like a year or two, which seems so unfair for how smart they are. I read somewhere that if they lived longer, they could probably take over the world. Honestly, they'd probably do a better job than we are. But no, to answer your question, the anxiety is just about what happened with my husband.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel physically on edge or notice things like trouble sleeping, irritability, or being easily startled, especially since the accident?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sleep is... it's really bad right now. Most nights I just lie there replaying things in my head for an hour or two before I even drift off. And even when I finally do fall asleep, I wake up at every little creak the house makes, or I have these awful dreams where I hear him hit the ground again. It's miserable. I actually tried drinking some chamomile tea before bed, thinking it might help. My neighbor, Mrs. Higgins, swears by it. She grows her own chamomile in this little garden box on her patio. She's got quite the green thumb, honestly. She grew these massive heirloom tomatoes last summer that she shared with us—they were so good, just sliced with a little salt and olive oil. I tried growing tomatoes once, but the squirrels got to every single one of them before they even ripened. They are so bold! I saw one sitting on my fence just staring at me while it ate a strawberry right out of my planter. I bought this supposedly squirrel-proof bird feeder, but they figured it out in like two days. They're like little acrobats. I watched a video online about this guy who built this elaborate obstacle course for the squirrels in his yard, and they just breezed right through it. But anyway, yeah, the sleeping thing is a real problem since the accident. I'm just so on edge all night.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever notice feelings of anxiety or fear coming up at times when your mind is completely off the accident and not thinking about it at all?\n\nPatient: No, I really don't. Like I was saying, it's pretty much just when I'm thinking about the porch or him falling. If my mind is actually off it, which is rare, I feel okay. Like the other day, I was at the grocery store, just trying to find this specific brand of mustard my husband likes. It's this stone-ground one with the little seeds in it. I don't really get the appeal, honestly, I'm more of a classic yellow mustard person. Or maybe honey mustard if I'm having chicken nuggets. I haven't had chicken nuggets in forever. There used to be this place near my old apartment that made the best homemade chicken nuggets, they were huge. The guy who owned it was this really grumpy older man, but he made amazing food. He closed down during the pandemic, though, which is such a shame. A lot of good places closed down then. It's crazy how much the neighborhood has changed just in the last few years. All these new coffee shops popping up everywhere, charging like seven dollars for a latte. I mean, I like a good latte, but that's just ridiculous. I bought an espresso machine for my house thinking it would save me money, but it's so complicated to use. It takes me twenty minutes just to make one cup, and then I have to clean the little milk frother thing, which is a huge pain. So it just sits on my counter taking up space. But yeah, no, if I'm not thinking about the accident, I don't just feel anxious for no reason.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any anxiety or fear coming up in situations that are completely unrelated to the accident, even when your mind isn’t on it at all?\n\nPatient: Honestly, doctor, I feel like you've asked me this three times now. But no, I really don't. It's just about the accident. If I'm not thinking about it, I'm fine. I mean, as fine as I can be. I was actually just telling my sister this on the phone yesterday. She called me while I was trying to organize my hall closet. It's been a disaster for months. We have all these winter coats shoved in there, and we live in a place where it only gets really cold for like, three weeks out of the year. My husband has this massive puffer jacket that takes up half the space, and he refuses to get rid of it because he wore it on a ski trip we took before we got married. I don't even like skiing. It's so cold, and the boots are incredibly uncomfortable. Plus, I'm terrified of breaking a leg. I broke my arm when I was eight falling off the monkey bars, and having a cast in the middle of summer was the worst experience of my life. It itched so badly, and my mom wouldn't let me stick a ruler down there to scratch it. She said I'd get an infection. Moms are always so paranoid about that stuff. But anyway, to answer your question again, no, I don't have random anxiety when I'm not thinking about what happened.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Are we... are we stuck on a loop today, doctor? Because I swear I just answered this. No, I really don't. It's strictly just about the fall. If my mind isn't on that, I'm not anxious. 

I mean, you'd think I would be, given my job. I'm a building inspector, so my whole day is literally looking for things that are dangerous or built wrong. Like on Tuesday, I was at this new commercial site downtown, and the framing was an absolute disaster. The general contractor—this guy Gary, who always wears these ridiculous neon orange safety glasses indoors, which makes zero sense—tried to tell me the load-bearing wall was up to code. But I could see it bowing from ten feet away. I had to red-tag the whole project. He was furious, his face turned the exact same color as his glasses. It's wild how much people try to cut corners lately, especially with lumber prices being what they are. 

My cousin Mark tried to build a deck in his backyard last summer and ended up spending like triple his original budget. He finally just switched to that composite decking stuff. It actually looks really nice, and you don't have to worry about splinters. I got a huge splinter under my fingernail once when I was a kid, and my dad had to dig it out with a sewing needle. It hurt so bad, I think I cried for an hour. 

But anyway... what was I saying? Right, no. If I'm not thinking about the porch or him falling, I'm not just randomly scared of other things.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Okay, seriously, is this a trick question? Because I feel like I'm taking crazy pills here. I really, truly don't get random anxiety. It is strictly about the fall. 

You asking me the exact same thing over and over kind of reminds me of my grandma's old record player. It had this deep scratch on her favorite Fleetwood Mac vinyl, and it would just skip and play the same three seconds of "Rhiannon" on a loop until you physically bumped the console table it was sitting on. I actually ended up inheriting that table when she passed. It's this gorgeous mid-century modern teak thing, but I had to completely sand it down and refinish it because my uncle left a giant water ring on it from a potted plant. Who puts a damp potted plant directly on bare vintage wood without a coaster or a tray? It completely ruined the top layer. I spent like three entire weekends out in my garage breathing in polyurethane fumes trying to fix it. I probably should have worn a better respirator, honestly. With my job, I definitely know better, but I just wanted to get it done before we hosted Thanksgiving. 

But yeah, to be crystal clear: no. If I am not thinking about the accident, I am not anxious.

My mom always said I carry the weight of the world on my shoulders. She actually just retired and moved down to Florida, which is nice for her, she got this cute little condo near the beach... but yeah, I just can't shake this awful feeling.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way? Have these feelings of tension, fear, and guilt been constant, or did they start around the time of these recent job-related stressors?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, it wasn't the job. I mean, work has always been a headache, but this is different. This has been going on for about four months, ever since my husband's fall during the porch repair. We were fixing up the back deck because we bought this older Craftsman house a couple of years ago, and it has all this gorgeous original woodwork, but honestly, it’s been such a money pit. The previous owners just let the weather completely destroy the back steps, and we’ve been trying to do the repairs ourselves on the weekends to save some money. Have you seen the price of lumber lately? It is absolutely ridiculous. We went to the hardware store for just a few boards of treated pine and I couldn't believe the total at the register. But yeah, anyway... it's been four months. Ever since he fell out there, I just haven't been able to shake this feeling.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your worry or anxiety extends beyond just work or your husband’s accident—like if you're feeling anxious about a lot of different things, even when there isn’t a clear reason?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say it's about everything. It's really just tied to what happened. I don't sit around worrying about, you know, getting a flat tire or whether the bills are paid on time. We actually just switched our car insurance because they kept hiking the rates up every six months for no reason. My husband finally called them and argued with the guy for like twenty minutes, but they wouldn't budge. So we went with a different company and ended up saving like forty bucks a month, which is great because we really need to replace the gutters before winter. The leaves from the neighbor's oak tree just completely clog them up every single fall, and I swear, that tree drops more branches than leaves sometimes. But no, I'm not just anxious about random things. It's just... this heavy feeling about the accident.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about the accident and whether you could have done something differently, even when you’re not actively thinking about it?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I do worry about that a lot. I mean, it's not just about the accident itself, but what it means about me. I keep thinking that if I missed something so obvious at my own house, then maybe I'm not as careful or competent as I thought I was. Which is crazy, because my work reports are always spotless. I mean, my supervisor, Dave, he just did my annual review last month and gave me the highest marks in attention to detail. Dave's a good guy, actually, he just had his first grandbaby—a little girl. He showed me pictures on his phone the other day, and she was wearing this tiny little knitted hat that his wife made. She knits all kinds of things, apparently she sells them at the farmer's market on weekends. I've been meaning to go check out that market, they're supposed to have really good local honey, and I've heard local honey is great for seasonal allergies. My allergies have been acting up so bad lately with all the ragweed. But anyway, yeah, even though my work is fine, I just have this awful belief now that I'm somehow failing at being safe.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any trouble focusing or staying concentrated on tasks lately, even ones you usually handle without a problem?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. My focus is actually pretty good. I mean, as a building inspector, I have to be pretty dialed in, right? Like, I was just out at that new mixed-use development they're putting up over on 4th Street the other day. Have you seen the traffic around there lately? It is an absolute nightmare. They blocked off two whole lanes just for the cement trucks, and the detour signs make zero sense. I ended up stuck behind this huge delivery truck for like twenty minutes, and the driver was just sitting there scrolling on his phone the whole time. It's crazy how glued to their screens people are now. My sister just got my niece a smartphone for her tenth birthday, which honestly just seems way too young to me. When we were ten, we were outside riding our bikes around the neighborhood until the streetlights came on, you know? We used to put those little plastic beads on the spokes that made noise when you pedaled. But yeah, anyway, my concentration at work and stuff hasn't been an issue at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself avoiding certain places, people, or activities because they remind you of your husband's fall?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I guess I have been avoiding some things. Like at work, I've started turning down jobs that have raised porches or second-story decks. I just... I can't do it right now. Which is annoying because the weather has been so perfect for outdoor inspections lately. We had that string of days last week where it was just sunny and 70 degrees, like absolute perfection. I love that kind of weather, it reminds me of this trip my husband and I took to San Diego a few years ago. We stayed at this little Airbnb near the beach, and the host left us this basket of local snacks. There were these incredible chili-lime macadamia nuts in there—I've actually tried looking for them online since we got back, but I can never find the exact brand. I did find a recipe to make them at home, but honestly, who has the time to roast their own nuts? Especially with how expensive groceries are right now. I went to the store just to grab eggs and milk yesterday and it was like twenty dollars. But anyway, at home, I also just find excuses not to talk about the repair or even look at the spot where he fell. I just try to keep busy with other stuff so I don't have to deal with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling guilty or blaming yourself for your husband's fall, even though you know it may not have been entirely your fault?\n\nPatient: Yeah, the guilt is definitely there. I mean, that's really what brings me in, honestly. I just can't shake this heavy mix of fear and guilt, like I'm just tense all day waiting for something else to go wrong, and I keep blaming myself for not stopping what happened. It's exhausting. I was telling my friend Sarah about it—she's a nurse over at the county hospital, actually. She works in the pediatric ward, which I always tell her I could never do. Kids are just so fragile, you know? She was telling me about this little boy who came in last week who swallowed a bunch of those magnetic toy beads. Can you believe they even still sell those? I swear, half the toys they make nowadays seem like hazards. When I was a kid, my favorite toy was this wooden train set my grandfather carved for me. He was amazing with woodworking. He had this whole shop set up in his garage with a lathe and everything. The smell of sawdust always reminds me of him. He used to make these intricate little birdhouses, too. I actually have one hanging in my backyard right now, and we get the most beautiful blue jays visiting it. They're so loud, though, especially early in the morning. But yeah, the guilt is just... it's a lot to carry around all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your guilt and fear are directly related to the accident and your role in it, or do you sometimes feel this way even when you're not thinking about the accident?\n\nPatient: I mean, it's definitely tied to the accident. I don't really walk around feeling guilty about other stuff, you know? It's just... it's about him falling and me not fixing that railing when I should have. Which is crazy because I literally inspect buildings for a living. I should have known better. I did this inspection on a commercial property last month, this old warehouse they're converting into lofts. The amount of code violations was unbelievable. They had exposed wiring just hanging right next to the water pipes. I was talking to the general contractor, and he was trying to tell me it was \"temporary.\" Like, buddy, temporary or not, it's a fire hazard. I actually had a small grease fire in my kitchen once, years ago. Scared the life out of me. I was trying to fry some chicken, which I never do, but I'd watched this cooking show the night before and got inspired. The host made it look so easy. Have you ever noticed how cooking shows always have all the ingredients perfectly chopped in those little glass bowls before they even start? It's like, of course cooking is relaxing when you have an entire prep team behind the scenes. I bought a set of those little glass bowls thinking it would make me more organized, but they just end up taking up space in my dishwasher. I hate unloading the dishwasher, honestly. It's my least favorite chore. But yeah, the fear and the guilt... it's all just about the accident. It's not just a general thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or fearful even when you're not thinking about the accident or anything related to it?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's pretty much just tied to the accident. If I'm not thinking about the porch or him falling, I don't just sit around feeling scared out of nowhere. I mean, unless I'm watching a scary movie or something, but I don't really do that anymore. I used to love horror movies when I was a teenager. My friends and I would rent those cheesy slasher films from Blockbuster—God, remember Blockbuster? Getting to walk down those aisles on a Friday night, smelling the plastic cases and the popcorn. It was such a ritual. Now everything is just streaming, and you spend more time scrolling through options than actually watching anything. I tried to find something to watch on Netflix the other night and gave up after twenty minutes. I ended up just watching this documentary about octopuses instead. Did you know they have three hearts? It's fascinating. But they only live for like a year or two, which seems so unfair for how smart they are. I read somewhere that if they lived longer, they could probably take over the world. Honestly, they'd probably do a better job than we are. But no, to answer your question, the anxiety is just about what happened with my husband.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel physically on edge or notice things like trouble sleeping, irritability, or being easily startled, especially since the accident?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sleep is... it's really bad right now. Most nights I just lie there replaying things in my head for an hour or two before I even drift off. And even when I finally do fall asleep, I wake up at every little creak the house makes, or I have these awful dreams where I hear him hit the ground again. It's miserable. I actually tried drinking some chamomile tea before bed, thinking it might help. My neighbor, Mrs. Higgins, swears by it. She grows her own chamomile in this little garden box on her patio. She's got quite the green thumb, honestly. She grew these massive heirloom tomatoes last summer that she shared with us—they were so good, just sliced with a little salt and olive oil. I tried growing tomatoes once, but the squirrels got to every single one of them before they even ripened. They are so bold! I saw one sitting on my fence just staring at me while it ate a strawberry right out of my planter. I bought this supposedly squirrel-proof bird feeder, but they figured it out in like two days. They're like little acrobats. I watched a video online about this guy who built this elaborate obstacle course for the squirrels in his yard, and they just breezed right through it. But anyway, yeah, the sleeping thing is a real problem since the accident. I'm just so on edge all night.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever notice feelings of anxiety or fear coming up at times when your mind is completely off the accident and not thinking about it at all?\n\nPatient: No, I really don't. Like I was saying, it's pretty much just when I'm thinking about the porch or him falling. If my mind is actually off it, which is rare, I feel okay. Like the other day, I was at the grocery store, just trying to find this specific brand of mustard my husband likes. It's this stone-ground one with the little seeds in it. I don't really get the appeal, honestly, I'm more of a classic yellow mustard person. Or maybe honey mustard if I'm having chicken nuggets. I haven't had chicken nuggets in forever. There used to be this place near my old apartment that made the best homemade chicken nuggets, they were huge. The guy who owned it was this really grumpy older man, but he made amazing food. He closed down during the pandemic, though, which is such a shame. A lot of good places closed down then. It's crazy how much the neighborhood has changed just in the last few years. All these new coffee shops popping up everywhere, charging like seven dollars for a latte. I mean, I like a good latte, but that's just ridiculous. I bought an espresso machine for my house thinking it would save me money, but it's so complicated to use. It takes me twenty minutes just to make one cup, and then I have to clean the little milk frother thing, which is a huge pain. So it just sits on my counter taking up space. But yeah, no, if I'm not thinking about the accident, I don't just feel anxious for no reason.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any anxiety or fear coming up in situations that are completely unrelated to the accident, even when your mind isn’t on it at all?\n\nPatient: Honestly, doctor, I feel like you've asked me this three times now. But no, I really don't. It's just about the accident. If I'm not thinking about it, I'm fine. I mean, as fine as I can be. I was actually just telling my sister this on the phone yesterday. She called me while I was trying to organize my hall closet. It's been a disaster for months. We have all these winter coats shoved in there, and we live in a place where it only gets really cold for like, three weeks out of the year. My husband has this massive puffer jacket that takes up half the space, and he refuses to get rid of it because he wore it on a ski trip we took before we got married. I don't even like skiing. It's so cold, and the boots are incredibly uncomfortable. Plus, I'm terrified of breaking a leg. I broke my arm when I was eight falling off the monkey bars, and having a cast in the middle of summer was the worst experience of my life. It itched so badly, and my mom wouldn't let me stick a ruler down there to scratch it. She said I'd get an infection. Moms are always so paranoid about that stuff. But anyway, to answer your question again, no, I don't have random anxiety when I'm not thinking about what happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt anxious or fearful in situations that aren’t related to your husband’s fall, even when your mind isn’t on the accident at all?\n\nPatient: Are we... are we stuck on a loop today, doctor? Because I swear I just answered this. No, I really don't. It's strictly just about the fall. If my mind isn't on that, I'm not anxious. \n\nI mean, you'd think I would be, given my job. I'm a building inspector, so my whole day is literally looking for things that are dangerous or built wrong. Like on Tuesday, I was at this new commercial site downtown, and the framing was an absolute disaster. The general contractor—this guy Gary, who always wears these ridiculous neon orange safety glasses indoors, which makes zero sense—tried to tell me the load-bearing wall was up to code. But I could see it bowing from ten feet away. I had to red-tag the whole project. He was furious, his face turned the exact same color as his glasses. It's wild how much people try to cut corners lately, especially with lumber prices being what they are. \n\nMy cousin Mark tried to build a deck in his backyard last summer and ended up spending like triple his original budget. He finally just switched to that composite decking stuff. It actually looks really nice, and you don't have to worry about splinters. I got a huge splinter under my fingernail once when I was a kid, and my dad had to dig it out with a sewing needle. It hurt so bad, I think I cried for an hour. \n\nBut anyway... what was I saying? Right, no. If I'm not thinking about the porch or him falling, I'm not just randomly scared of other things.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt anxious or fearful in situations that are completely unrelated to your husband's fall, even when your mind isn’t on the accident at all?\n\nPatient: Okay, seriously, is this a trick question? Because I feel like I'm taking crazy pills here. I really, truly don't get random anxiety. It is strictly about the fall. \n\nYou asking me the exact same thing over and over kind of reminds me of my grandma's old record player. It had this deep scratch on her favorite Fleetwood Mac vinyl, and it would just skip and play the same three seconds of \"Rhiannon\" on a loop until you physically bumped the console table it was sitting on. I actually ended up inheriting that table when she passed. It's this gorgeous mid-century modern teak thing, but I had to completely sand it down and refinish it because my uncle left a giant water ring on it from a potted plant. Who puts a damp potted plant directly on bare vintage wood without a coaster or a tray? It completely ruined the top layer. I spent like three entire weekends out in my garage breathing in polyurethane fumes trying to fix it. I probably should have worn a better respirator, honestly. With my job, I definitely know better, but I just wanted to get it done before we hosted Thanksgiving. \n\nBut yeah, to be crystal clear: no. If I am not thinking about the accident, I am not anxious."
